Relatively non-degenerate integrated decay estimates on subextremal Kerr–de Sitter
Georgios Mavrogiannis

Abstract
. We study the Klein–Gordon equation □ ​ ψ − μ KG 2 ​ ψ = 0 \Box\psi-\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}\psi=0 on subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole backgrounds with parameters ( a , M , l ) (a,M,l) , where l 2 = 3 Λ l^{2}=\frac{3}{\Lambda} . We prove a ‘relatively non-degenerate integrated decay estimate’ assuming an appropriate mode stability statement for real frequency solutions of Carter’s radial ode. Our results, in particular, apply unconditionally in the very slowly rotating case | a | ≪ M , l |a|\ll M,l , and in the case where ψ \psi is axisymmetric. Exponential decay for ψ \psi to a constant is a consequence of this estimate. To prove our result, we introduce a novel pseudodifferential commutation operator 𝒢 \mathcal{G} that generalizes our previous purely physical space commutation [ 31 ] and we use it in conjunction with the Morawetz estimate of our companion [ 28 ] . This pseudodifferential operator is defined using Fourier decomposition with respect to time frequencies ω \omega and azimuthal frequencies m m , but does not require Carter’s full separation.



1.
Introduction

Let
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M,l})
, with
l^{2}=\frac{3}{\Lambda}
, be a Kerr–de Sitter black hole spacetime, where the metric in Boyer–Lindquist coordinates (see
[
6
]
) takes the form

(1.1)

\displaystyle g_{a,M,l}=

\displaystyle\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Delta}dr^{2}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Delta_{\theta}}d\theta^{2}+\frac{\Delta_{\theta}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-\Delta a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Xi^{2}\rho^{2}}\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2}-2\frac{\Delta_{\theta}(r^{2}+a^{2})-\Delta}{\Xi\rho^{2}}a\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi dt

\displaystyle-\frac{\Delta-\Delta_{\theta}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\rho^{2}}dt^{2},

with
\Xi=1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}},\penalty\ \Delta_{\theta}=1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\cos^{2}\theta,\penalty\ \rho^{2}=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta
, where

(1.2)

\Delta=(r^{2}+a^{2})\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{l^{2}}\right)-2Mr.

In the non-rotating case
a=0
the Kerr–de Sitter metric (
1.1
) reduces to the Schwarzschild–de Sitter metric

(1.3)

g_{M,l}=-\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}\right)dt^{2}+\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}\right)^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}},

where
d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
is the standard metric of the unit sphere.

In our previous
[
31
]
we studied the wave equation

(1.4)

\Box_{g_{M,l}}\psi=0

on the Schwarzschild–de Sitter black hole spacetime (
1.3
). Specifically, we proved a ‘relatively non-degenerate integrated energy decay estimate’ by commuting (
1.4
) with the vector field
\mathcal{G}=r\sqrt{1-\frac{2M}{r}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}}\partial_{r}
, here written in specially chosen hyperboloidal coordinates. Note that Holzegel–Kauffman
[
20
]
initially studied an analogue of the vector field
\mathcal{G}
in the
\Lambda=0
Schwarzschild case, and proved a Morawetz estimate for the wave equation with small first order terms. Recently they extended their work in the subextremal Kerr case
[
21
]
. A relatively non degenerate integrated energy decay estimate controls a spacetime bulk term with degeneration at top order from an initial boundary flux term with the exact same degeneration, see already estimate (
1.8
) in Section
1.2
. Thus, the bulk control is non-degenerate relative to the boundary control. To prove this estimate we used in addition a Morawetz estimate of Dafermos–Rodnianski
[
9
]
.

In the present paper, we prove a ‘relatively non-degenerate’ integrated energy decay estimate (Theorem
1
) in the Kerr–de Sitter context. Specifically, we study solutions of the Klein–Gordon equation

(1.5)

\Box_{g_{a,M,l}}\psi-\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}\psi=0,

with mass
\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}\geq 0
(the case
\mu_{\textit{KG}}=0
will be called simply the ‘wave equation’) on a Kerr–de Sitter spacetime (
1.1
) with subextremal parameters
(a,M,l)
, under the following condition

\displaystyle\text{(MS):\penalty\ mode stability on the real axis for Carter's radial ode holds on a curve in subextremal}

\displaystyle\text{parameter space connecting}\penalty\ (a,M)\penalty\ \text{to the subextremal Schwarzschild--de\penalty\ Sitter family.}

The very slowly rotating case
|a|\ll M,l
is in fact included in the condition (MS), as shown in our companion
[
28
]
, where the smallness depends on
\mu_{KG}
. Theorem
1
also applies unconditionally to axisymmetric solutions of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.5
) for all
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
, in the full subextremal range of parameters.

To prove the theorem we introduce a novel pseudodifferential commutation operator
\mathcal{G}
, see already Section
1.4
, for the entire subextremal range of Kerr–de Sitter black holes, thus generalizing our previous work
[
31
]
on Schwarzschild–de Sitter, and use it in conjunction with a Morawetz estimate proved in our companion
[
28
]
. An immediate Corollary of our ‘relatively non-degenerate’ estimate (Theorem
1.8
) is the exponential decay for the solutions of the Klein–Gordon equation (Corollary
1.8
).

In contrast to our previous
[
31
]
, the commutation with
\mathcal{G}
is frequency dependent, see Section
5
, i.e. the operator
\mathcal{G}
is pseudodifferential and is defined applying the Fourier transform. The pseudodifferential aspect of
\mathcal{G}
only depends on the time frequency
\omega
and the aximuthal frequency
m
, which correspond to the Killing vector fields
\partial_{t},\partial_{\varphi}
respectively, but it does not require Carter’s full separation. As a result, in addition to initial boundary fluxes on the right hand side, the ‘relatively non-degenerate’ estimate of Theorem
1.8
contains an averaged bulk term, which is again however suitably localized in spacetime. There terms arise from cutoffs and pseudodifferential commutation error terms. Because
\mathcal{G}
is defined without recourse to Carter’s separation, these terms take a form which can be bounded by standard commutator estimates.

In addition, we prove the analogue of Theorem
1.8
for an inhomogeneous wave equation (Theorem
2
). In our forthcoming
[
29
]
we use Theorem
2
to give an elementary proof of stability and exponential decay of the solutions of appropriate quasilinear wave equations on a Kerr–de Sitter black hole background.

. We simply list some important references here.

\Lambda>0
see

,

,

,

,

]
. For the wave and Klein–Gordon equations in the case
\Lambda\leq 0
see for instance

,

,

.

[

,

,

,

,

]
further down the paper.

1.2.
The ‘relatively non-degenerate’ estimate of
[
31
]
on Schwarzschild–de Sitter

The present paper generalizes the commutation with

(1.6)

\mathcal{G}=r\sqrt{1-\frac{2M}{r}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}

of our previous
[
31
]
, which we here review. The vector field (
1.6
) is written in ‘special’ hyperboloidal coordinates
(\bar{t},r,\theta,\varphi)
on Schwarzschild–de Sitter which were specifically chosen to make the form of the vector field (
1.6
) simple. In particular, with our choice,
\mathcal{G}
is orthogonal to the timelike Killing field
\partial_{\bar{t}}
at
r=3M
and vanishes continuously (but not differentiably) at the horizons
\mathcal{H}^{+},\penalty\ \bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
. Note that in the standard Schwarzschild–de Sitter coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
the vector field (
1.6
) takes the form

(1.7)

\mathcal{G}=G_{1}(r)\partial_{r^{\star}}+G_{2}(r)\partial_{t},

where
r^{\star}
is the tortoise coordinate and

G_{1}(r)=\frac{r}{\sqrt{1-\frac{2M}{r}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}}},\qquad G_{2}(r)=\frac{r}{\sqrt{1-\frac{2M}{r}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}}}\left(1-\frac{3M}{r}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-9M^{2}\Lambda}}\sqrt{1+\frac{6M}{r}},

see
[
31
]
.

In
[
31
]
we studied the wave equation ((
1.5
) with
\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}=0
) on the Schwarzschild–de Sitter spacetime. Specifically, we commuted the wave equation with the vector field
\mathcal{G}
, see (
1.6
), which produced only one top order term, which had a ‘good’ sign. We applied an energy estimate for the equation satisfied by
\mathcal{G}\psi
, with multiplier
\partial_{\bar{t}}\mathcal{G}\psi
, and used the Morawetz estimate of Dafermos–Rodnianski, see
[
10
]
. In view of this ‘good’ sign, this led to a ‘relatively non-degenerate’ energy estimate

(1.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\{\bar{t}=\tau_{2}\}}\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}\psi,\psi)+\int\int_{\{\tau_{1}\leq\bar{t}\leq\tau_{2}\}}\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}\psi,\psi)\lesssim\int_{\{\bar{t}=\tau_{1}\}}\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}\psi,\psi),

for all
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
, where

(1.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}\psi,\psi)\sim(\partial_{\bar{t}}\mathcal{G}\psi)^{2}+\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}\right)(\partial_{r}\mathcal{G}\psi)^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\mathcal{G}\psi|^{2}+(\partial_{\bar{t}}\psi)^{2}+(\partial_{r}\psi)^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}.

The energy estimate of (
1.8
) is ‘relatively non-degenerate’ in the sense that in view of the coarea formula
\int\int_{\{\tau_{1}\leq\bar{t}\leq\tau_{2}\}}\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}\psi,\psi)\sim\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}d\tau\int_{\{\bar{t}=\tau\}}\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}\psi,\psi)
the boundary terms on the right hand side are precisely comparable to the bulk term on the left hand side.

Exponential decay followed immediately as a Corollary of (
1.8
).

Note that in
[
20
]
, Holzegel–Kauffman introduced a vector field conciding with (
1.7
) in the limit
\Lambda=0
, and used it to prove energy estimates for the wave equation with small first order error terms.

1.3.
A review of the results of our companion
[
28
]

In our companion
[
28
]
we prove boundedness and a degenerate Morawetz estimate for solutions of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.5
), after assuming (MS). We here review the Morawetz estimate of our companion
[
28
]
, where for the precise statement see already Theorem
4.1
.

The projection into Fourier space allows us to capture the phenomena of trapping and superadiance at the level of modes and moreover allows us to rigorously formulate our mode stability condition (MS). Moreover, we discuss Carter’s separation of variables for Kerr–de Sitter.

1.3.1.
The
(t,\varphi)
Fourier projections

Let
(a,M,l)
be subextremal. Let
\Psi
be a sufficiently regular with
\text{supp}\Psi\subset\{0\leq t^{\star}<\infty\}
, where for any
\tau\geq 0
the leaves
\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
connect the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
with the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
, see Section
2.2
. We define the Fourier projection

(1.10)

\mathcal{F}_{\omega,m}(\Psi)(r,\theta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{-i\omega t}e^{im\varphi}\Psi(t,r,\theta,\varphi)d\varphi dt,

where

(1.11)

\omega\in\mathbb{R},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z}

are the Fourier frequencies with respect to the Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
t,\varphi
respectively, see already Section
3
.

1.3.2.
The Hawking–Reall vector fields

We denote as

(1.12)

K_{+}=\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi},\qquad\bar{K}_{+}=\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi}

the Hawking–Reall vector fields of the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
respectively.

1.3.3.
The superradiant frequencies

Τhe
\bar{K}^{+}
energy flux along the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is given by

(1.13)

\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}K\psi\overline{\bar{K}^{+}\psi}=\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\left(\partial_{t}\psi+\omega_{+}\partial_{\varphi}\psi\right)\cdot\overline{\left(\partial_{t}\psi+\bar{\omega}_{+}\partial_{\varphi}\psi\right)}.

In particular, if we consider a solution of the form
\psi=e^{-i\omega t}e^{im\varphi}\psi_{0}(r,\theta)
then the sign of (
1.13
) is determined by the sign of

(1.14)

\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right).

The superradiant frequencies of Kerr–de Sitter are the following

(1.15)

\mathcal{SF}=\Bigl\{(\omega,m):\penalty\ am\omega\in\left(\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\Bigr\},\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{or equivalently}\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)<0,

where
r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}
correspond respectively to the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
.

1.3.4.
The radial ode in Kerr–de Sitter

Following
[
5
]
, also see
[
22
]
, we decompose the solution of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.5
) into Fourier modes (
1.11
) via (
1.10
) and then further into spheroidal harmonics. Remarkably, Carter’s separation of variables for the wave operator implies that the radial part
u
of the separated solution, for the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.5
), satisfies the ode

(1.16)

u^{\prime\prime}+(\omega^{2}-V)u=H

where
{}^{\prime}=\frac{d}{dr^{\star}}
, and
r^{\star}
is the tortoise coordinate, see Section
2.12
and
H
is a cut-off inhomogeneity. The potential
V
is real and takes the form

(1.17)

V=V_{0}+V_{\textit{SL}}+V_{\mu_{\textit{KG}}},\qquad V_{0}-\omega^{2}=\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}\left(\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}+(a\omega)^{2}-2m\omega a\Xi\right)-\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right)^{2},

where
V_{\textit{SL}},V_{\mu_{\textit{KG}}}
are frequency independent terms (the latter is associated with the Klein–Gordon mass) see Section
3
, and
\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
are the eigenvalues of the spherical part of Carter’s separation of variables, which are indexed by
\ell
.

1.3.5.
Energy identity and mode stability in Kerr–de Sitter

We say that a classical solution
u
of the radial inhomogeneous ode (
1.16
) satisfies outgoing boundary conditions at
r^{\star}=-\infty
,
r^{\star}=+\infty
if the following hold respectively

(1.18)

u^{\prime}=-i\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)u,\qquad u^{\prime}=i\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)u,

see already Section
3.5
.

For a classical solution
u
of (
1.16
) that satisfies both the outgoing boundary conditions (
1.18
) we multiply the radial ode (
1.16
) with
\bar{u}
and after integration by parts we obtain

(1.19)

\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)|u|^{2}(\infty)+\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)|u|^{2}(-\infty)=\imaginary(\bar{u}H).

For the superradiant frequencies (
1.15
), the left hand side of the energy identity (
1.19
) is not coercive. Therefore, we cannot conclude from (
1.19
) that there are no modes on the real axis. At present, it is not known whether mode stability holds for the solutions of the wave equation
\mu^{2}_{KG}=0
, or the conformally invariant wave equation
\mu^{2}_{KG}=\frac{2\Lambda}{3}
, on Kerr–de Sitter in the full subextremal case in the spirit of
[
46
,
39
]
. Therefore, we will need to appeal to condition (MS) (we do not expect that mode stability holds for all
\mu^{2}_{KG}>0
, see
[
38
]
). The very slowly rotating case
|a|\ll M
is in fact included in (MS), where the smallness depends on
\mu_{KG}
. Note, moreover, the partial mode stability result of Casals–Teixeira da Costa
[
7
]
and the recent result of Hintz
[
17
]
, which we will also discuss later.

1.3.6.
The boundedness and Morawetz estimates of
[
28
]

To prove our Morawetz estimate of
[
28
]
, quoted here as Theorem
4.1
, the work
[
28
]
followed the strategy of
[
10
]
and constructed fixed frequency multipliers for the radial ode (
1.16
). Note that two main features of the geometry of Kerr–de Sitter, trapping of null geodesics and superradiance, were also the main difficulties for proving the frequency localized estimates of
[
28
]
.

In Theorem
4.1
, we control from initial data the following quantity

(1.20)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\omega\sum_{m,\ell}\Big(\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}|u^{\prime}+i(\omega-\omega_{+}m)u|^{2}dr+\int_{\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon}^{\bar{r}_{+}}\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}|u^{\prime}-(\omega-\bar{\omega}_{+}m)u|^{2}dr

\displaystyle+\int_{r_{+}}^{\bar{r}_{+}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}dr+\left(1-\frac{r_{\textit{trap}}(\omega,m,\ell)}{r}\right)^{2}(\omega+\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell})|u|^{2}dr\Big)

for a sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, where
\omega_{+}=\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\penalty\ \bar{\omega}_{+}=\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
and where
u
is the fixed frequency solution of the radial ode (
1.16
).

Note that the integrand of (
1.20
) admits a degeneration in accordance with the obstructions of trapped null geodesics, see
[
36
,
40
]
. However, note that the precise degeneration of the integrand of (
1.20
) is at the frequency dependent values

(1.21)

r_{\textit{trap}}(\omega,m,\ell).

Furthermore, the boundedness estimate for solutions of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.5
) follows by using the Morawetz estimate in conjunction with some extra Fourier based arguments.

1.4.
The operator
\mathcal{G}

Having reviewed the necessary background, we now turn to discuss the generalization of the vector field (
1.6
) to the Kerr–de Sitter spacetime, which is an operator that we also denote as
\mathcal{G}
. Our generalized
\mathcal{G}
must be here pseudodifferential, depending on the frequencies
\omega,m
(as it also must capture the phenomenon of trapping on Kerr–de Sitter) but independent of the Carter frequency
\ell
.

Let
(\omega,m)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
. First, we define

(1.22)

\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r)=\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}{\Delta}\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right)^{2}

and

(1.23)

\displaystyle g_{1}(r^{\star}(r))\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\sqrt{\Delta}},\qquad\left(g_{2}(\omega,m,r)\right)^{2}=\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r)-\min_{r\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]}\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r),

where for
\Delta
see (
1.2
), with
\Xi=1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}
. Note that for the superradiant frequencies
(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{SF}
we have
\min_{r\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]}\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r)=0
.

Now, for any
(\omega,m)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
we denote as

(1.24)

r_{\textit{crit}}(\omega,m).

to be the unique critical point of
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r)
, see already Propositions
5.1
,
5.2
. Note that for the borderline non-superradiant frequencies

(1.25)

am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\qquad am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},

we have
r_{\textit{crit}}(\omega,m)=r_{+},r_{\textit{crit}}(\omega,m)=\bar{r}_{+}
.

Remark 1.1
.

(Connection of
\mathcal{G}
with trapping)
We note that when a trapped null geodesic exists, for certain conserved quantities
E,L
associated with
\omega
and
m
respectively, then the
r
-coordinate of the geodesic is
r_{crit}(E,L)
, where for
r_{crit}(\cdot,\cdot)
see (
1.24
). Therefore, our operator
\mathcal{G}
will indeed be connected to trapping. Specifically, note that in the Schwarzschild–de Sitter case we have
r_{crit}(\omega,m)=3M
for all
(\omega,m)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 1.1
.

We define a signed square root
g_{2}(\omega,m,r)
of
g_{2}^{2}(\omega,m,r)
defined by (
1.23
) as follows. For the frequencies
am\omega>\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
we define

(1.26)

g_{2}(\omega,m,r)=\begin{cases}-\sqrt{g_{2}^{2}(\omega,m,r)},\quad r\geq r_{\textit{crit}}(\omega,m)\\
+\sqrt{g_{2}^{2}(\omega,m,r)},\quad r\leq r_{\textit{crit}}(\omega,m).\end{cases}

For the superradiant frequencies
\mathcal{SF}
we define

(1.27)

g_{2}(\omega,m,r)=-\sqrt{g_{2}^{2}}=-\left|\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right|\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\sqrt{\Delta}}.

For the frequencies
am\omega<\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
we define

(1.28)

g_{2}(\omega,m,r)=\begin{cases}+\sqrt{g_{2}^{2}(\omega,m,r)},\quad r\geq r_{\textit{crit}}(\omega,m)\\
-\sqrt{g_{2}^{2}(\omega,m,r)},\quad r\leq r_{\textit{crit}}(\omega,m).\end{cases}

For the borderline non-superradiant frequencies
am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
we define respectively

(1.29)

g_{2}(\omega,m,r)=-\sqrt{g_{2}^{2}}=-|am\Xi|\frac{r^{2}-r_{+}^{2}}{(r_{+}^{2}+a^{2})\sqrt{\Delta}},\qquad g_{2}(\omega,m,r)=-\sqrt{g_{2}^{2}}=-|am\Xi|\frac{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}-r^{2}}{(\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2})\sqrt{\Delta}}.

In the Schwarzschild–de Sitter case
a=0
we define
g_{2}=g_{1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-9M^{2}\Lambda}}\left(1-\frac{3M}{r}\right)\sqrt{1+\frac{6M}{r}}
, as in our previous
[
31
]
. For a graphic presentation of
g_{2}(\omega,m,r)
see already Figure
1
.

We now present the rough definition of the operator
\mathcal{G}
, which is a generalization of the vector field
\mathcal{G}
of
[
31
]
. For the precise definition of
\mathcal{G}
see already Section
5
.

Definition
1

(rough version)
.

Let
g_{1}(r),\penalty\ g_{2}(\omega,m,r)
be as in (
1.23
) and Definition
1.1
. We define an operator
\mathcal{G}
of the form

(1.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mug_{1}(r^{\star})\partial_{r^{\star}}+i\textit{Op}\left(g_{2}\right),

where
\textit{Op}(g_{2})
is a Fourier multiplier operator defined as follows

(1.31)

\left(\textit{Op}(g_{2})\Psi\right)(r;t,\varphi,\theta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\omega\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{i\omega t}e^{-im\varphi}g_{2}(\omega,m,r)\mathcal{F}_{\omega,m}(\Psi)(r,\theta),

where for
\mathcal{F}_{\omega,m}
see (
1.10
).

Remark 1.2
.

For the superradiant frequencies
\mathcal{SF}
we note that there exists an
r_{s}\in(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
such that
\omega=\frac{am\Xi}{r_{s}^{2}+a^{2}}
. Then, the function
g_{2}(\frac{am\Xi}{r_{s}^{2}+a^{2}},m,\cdot)
is not differentiable at
r=r_{s}
. For a graphic representation of this non-differentiability see already Figure
1
. Furthermore, for any fixed
r\in(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
and for any any fixed
m
such that
(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{SF}
we have that
g_{2}(\cdot,m,r)
is not a differentiable function either. Therefore, in order to be able to use pseudodifferential commutations, we will approximate
\mathcal{G}
by a more regular version, which we denote as
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
, see already Definition
5.4
. The pseudodifferential operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
belongs in the class of operators satisfying the assumptions of the classical Coifman–Meyer commutation Lemma
A.1
. Introducing the more regular operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
is essential in the pseudodifferential estimates of Section
8
.

Remark 1.3
.

The operator
\mathcal{G}
of Definition
1
can also be defined in the asymptotically flat case
\Lambda=0
. In the high frequency limit our relevant
\Lambda=0
operator is related to but differs from the relevant commutator employed in the recent work of Holzegel–Kauffman
[
21
]
.

We note finally that our commutation with
\mathcal{G}
, see (
1.30
), in the high frequency limit, connects with the previous works
[
1
,
4
,
25
,
33
,
18
,
14
,
13
]
.

1.5.
The function
g_{2}(\omega,m,r)
graphically

In Figure
1
we graph the function
g_{2}(\omega,m,r)
, see Definition
1.4
, for the values

(1.32)

am\omega>\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\quad am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\quad am\omega\in\left(\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right),\quad am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\quad am\omega<\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}

respectively, where for
r_{\textit{crit}}(\omega,m)
see (
1.24
).

Figure 1.
The function
g_{2}(\omega,m,r)
for the values (
1.32
) respectively

1.6.
A bulk integral associated with
g_{1}\frac{d}{dr^{\star}}+ig_{2}

As mentioned already, we note that in the definition of the pseudodifferential operator
\mathcal{G}
only refers to frequencies
\omega,m
and not the full Carter separation of variables (
1.16
). We will employ extensively in this paper, however, the formalism of the Carter separation, since we can use the fixed freqency multiplier formalism of
[
28
]
and borrow all relevant formulas (see already (
1.33
)). In addition, we need the Morawetz estimate with the sharp degeneration at
r_{trap}(\omega,m,\ell)
(and not simply with a degeneration at an open interval), where
\ell
is the Carter frequency, see Theorem
4.1
. Furthermore, the Carter separation also allows us to formulate mode stability.

Let
g_{1},g_{2}
be as in (
1.23
) and Definition
1.1
, and let

(1.33)

v=\left(g_{1}\frac{d}{dr^{\star}}+ig_{2}\right)u

where
u
is the fixed frequency Fourier projection of
\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}\chi\psi
and
\chi
is an appropriate cut-off. We have the following Parseval identity

(1.34)

\int_{r_{+}}^{\bar{r}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{m,\ell}|v|^{2}drd\omega=\int_{r_{+}}^{\bar{r}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\mathcal{G}\left(\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}\chi\psi\right)|^{2}r^{2}drdtd\sigma,

where
d\sigma
is the standard metric of the unit sphere.

The main point of our choice of
\mathcal{G}
is to control the following fixed frequency expression

(1.35)

\int_{r_{+}}^{\bar{r}_{+}}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta}\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right)^{2}|v|^{2}+\tilde{\lambda}|v|^{2}+|v^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{1}{\Delta}|v^{\prime}+i(\omega-\omega_{+}m)v|^{2}+\frac{1}{\Delta}|v^{\prime}-(\omega-\bar{\omega}_{+}m)v|^{2}\right)dr,

where
\omega_{+}=\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\penalty\ \bar{\omega}_{+}=\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
and
\tilde{\lambda}
is associated with the eigenvalues of the angular ode, see already Section
3
. Note that in contrast to the expression (
1.20
), the expression (
1.35
) does not degenerate at top order at trapping
r=r_{\textit{trap}}

with respect to
v
.

The key ingredient in controlling the quantity (
1.35
) is to prove that passing to
v
defined by the commutation (
1.33
) produces a favorable term for all
(\omega,m)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
. Motivated by the relevant approach of our previous
[
31
]
and in view of the fact that in Kerr–de Sitter there does not exist a global timelike Killing vector field, for non-superradiant frequencies
(\omega,m)\in(\mathcal{SF})^{c}
we multiply the commuted equation, the equation satisfied by
v
, with the multiplier
(|\omega|+|am\Xi|)\bar{v}
and we generate the term

(1.36)

2\frac{g_{2}^{\prime}}{g_{1}}|v|^{2}

which controls the first term of (
1.35
). Also, note from Figure
1
that for the non-superradiant frequencies the function
g_{2}
is monotonic. To generate the remaining terms of (
1.35
) we use the ode satisfied by
v
, see already Lemma
7.1
.

The treatment of the superradiant frequencies
(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{SF}
is slightly more elaborate. Note that for
(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{SF}
there exists an
r_{s}(\omega,m)\in(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
such that
am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{s}^{2}+a^{2}}
. Specifically, we multiply the equation satisfied by
v
with a multiplier of the form
f_{s}(\omega,m,r)|am\Xi|\bar{v}
, where the smooth function
f_{s}(\omega,m,r)
degenerates as follows
f_{s}(\omega,m,r_{s})=0
. Then, we obtain an estimate where the LHS degenerates at the value
r_{s}
. In order to ‘cover’ that degeneration we need to appeal to a quantitative manifestation of the fact that superradiant frequencies are not trapped, see also our companion
[
28
]
, and also see
[
10
]
for the manifestation of that phaenomenon in the asymptotically flat Kerr geometry.

We emphasize here that although we will use the formalism of Carter’s separation to produce our multiplier estimates, all of our multipliers will be independent of the Carter frequency
\ell
.

All lower order terms produced are controlled by the Morawetz estimate of our companion
[
28
]
, see Theorem
4.1
, since this estimate controls the quantity (
1.20
), in conjunction with a Poincare type estimate, see already Lemma
7.5
.

1.7.
The time cut-offs and the energy density

Let
(t^{\star},r,\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})
denote the Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates, for the precise definition see already Section
2.2
, where note that the leaves
\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
connect the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
with the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
.

In view of the pseudodifferential nature of the operator
\mathcal{G}
, see Definition
1.4
, to make the statement of the main Theorem
1
to be of local nature we need to first apply appropriate cut-offs. Let
T>0
and
T<T+\tau_{1}<2T+\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}
. We choose a smooth cut-off that satisfies the following

(1.37)

\chi^{(T)}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}(t^{\star})=\begin{cases}\text{1,}&\quad\{\tau_{1}+T\leq t^{\star}\leq\tau_{2}-T\}\\
\text{0,}&\quad\{t^{\star}\leq\tau_{1}\}\cup\{t^{\star}\geq\tau_{2}\},\\
\end{cases}

which we often simply denote as
\chi_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}
.

Let
T\geq 3
, let

(1.38)

\tilde{T}=T^{2}-2T-1

and let
\tilde{T}\leq\tau_{1}
. We define

(1.39)

\chi_{+}(t^{\star})=\chi^{(T)}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{1}+T^{2}}(t^{\star}),\qquad\chi_{-}(t^{\star})=\chi^{(T)}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{1}+T^{2}}\left(t^{\star}+\tilde{T}\right),

also see Figure
2
, where note that we supress the dependence of
\chi_{\pm}
on
T
and
\tau_{1}
.

Figure 2.
The cut-offs (
1.39
)

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}=\chi\mathcal{G}\chi
, where
\mathcal{G}
is as in (
1.30
) and
\chi
is either
\chi_{+}
or
\chi_{-}
, see (
1.39
). We define the energy density

(1.40)

\mathcal{E}\left(\mathcal{G}_{\chi}\psi,\psi\right)\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\mathbb{T}(\partial_{t^{\star}}+\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi^{\star}},n)[\mathcal{G}_{\chi}\psi]+\mathbb{T}(n,n)[\psi],

where
\mathbb{T}
is the energy momentum tensor of the Klein–Gordon equation, see already Section
2
, and
n
is the unit normal to the foliation
\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
. The vector field

(1.41)

\partial_{t^{\star}}+\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi^{\star}}

is globally timelike except at the horizons
\mathcal{H}^{+},\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
where it is null, see already Lemma
2.2
. Moreover, note that the energy density (
1.40
) satisfies

(1.42)

\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{\chi}\psi,\psi)\sim\left|\partial_{t^{\star}}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}\psi\right|^{2}+\Delta\left|Z^{\star}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}\psi\right|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\mathcal{G}_{\chi}\psi|^{2}+|\partial_{t^{\star}}\psi|^{2}+|Z^{\star}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2},

where for
\Delta
see (
1.2
), and for the regular vector field
Z^{\star}
see already Section
2.5
.

1.8.
The rough version of Theorem
1

We present the rough version of our ‘relatively non-degenerate’ estimate, where for the detailed version of Theorem
1
see already Section
6
.

Theorem
1

(rough version)
.

Let
(a,M,l)
be subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole parameters and let
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. Let
T\geq 3
be sufficiently large and let
\tilde{T}<\tau_{1}<\tau_{1}+T<\tau_{2}
, where for
\tilde{T}
see (
1.38
). Assume that

\displaystyle\text{(MS):\penalty\ mode stability on the real axis for Carter's radial ode holds on a curve in subextremal}

\displaystyle\text{parameter space connecting}\penalty\ (a,M)\penalty\ \text{to the subextremal Schwarzschild--de\penalty\ Sitter family},

and let
\psi
be a solution of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.5
) on the subextremal Kerr-de Sitter black hole with parameters
(a,M,l)
.

Then, the following ‘relatively non-degenerate’ integrated decay energy estimate holds

(1.43)

\displaystyle\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}}\mathbb{T}(n,n)[\psi]+\int\int_{\{\tau_{1}+T\leq t^{\star}\leq\tau_{2}\}}\mathcal{E}\left(\mathcal{G}_{\chi_{+}}\psi,\psi\right)\lesssim\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}\mathbb{T}(n,n)[\psi]+\frac{1}{T}\int\int_{\{\tau_{1}\leq t^{\star}\leq\tau_{1}+T\}}\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{\chi_{-}}\psi,\psi),

where for the smooth cut-offs
\chi_{+},\chi_{-}
see (
1.39
) and Figure
2
, and for the operator
\mathcal{G}
see (
1.30
). The constant implicit in (
1.43
) is independent of
T
.

For axisymmetric solutions
\psi
of the Klein–Gordon equation we obtain unconditionally a physical space version of (
1.43
) for all subextremal black hole parameters
(a,M,l)
and all masses
\mu_{KG}^{2}\geq 0
.

Remark 1.4
.

For fixed
(a,M,l)
, the constants implicit in
\lesssim
, in the RHS of the estimate (
1.43
), blow up in the limit
\mu_{KG}\rightarrow 0
as well as in the limit
a\rightarrow 0
. Note, however, that
\mu_{KG}=0
or
a=0
are indeed allowed in the theorem, i.e. the constant for
\mu_{KG}=0
or for
a=0
are finite. It might be possible to modify our proof, see specifically Section
8.10
(for the issue in the limit
\mu^{2}_{KG}\rightarrow 0
) and Section
8.7
(for the issue in the limit
a\rightarrow 0
), in order to obtain uniform estimates near
\mu^{2}_{KG}=0
and near
a=0
, which would be more desirable.

Remark 1.5
.

As mentioned in our companion
[
28
]
the very slowly rotating case
|a|\ll M,l
is in fact included in the condition (MS), where the smallnes depends on
\mu_{KG}
. Therefore, Theorem
1
holds for very slowly rotating Kerr–de Sitter black holes, as well. In view of the recent work of Hintz
[
17
]
, for
\mu^{2}_{KG}=0
, we have that subetremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole parameters with sufficiently small cosmological constant
0<|a|<M\ll l
are in fact included in the set (MS), where the constant implicit in
\ll
depends on the difference
M-|a|
. Therefore, the result of Theorem
1
holds also in this case.

Remark 1.6
.

In fact we can control the stronger quantity (
1.35
), summed over frequencies, on the left hand side of our main estimate (
1.43
).

Remark 1.7
.

Unlike the ‘relatively non-degenerate’ estimate (
1.8
) in Schwarzschild–de Sitter, the estimate of Theorem
1.8
exhibits an additional averaged bulk term on the right hand side. This relates to the fact that the operator
\mathcal{G}
, see Definition
1.4
, is of pseudodifferential nature. Nonetheless, because of the time translational relation of the cut-offs
\chi_{-},\penalty\ \chi_{+}
and the weight
\frac{1}{T}
, the estimate of Theorem
1.8
can similarly be immediately iterated in consecutive spacetime slabs of fixed time length in order to prove exponential decay in energy for the solutions of the Klein–Gordon equation, see Corollary
1.8
.

Exponential decay is an immediate Corollary by iterating the result of Theorem
1.8
in consecutive spacetime slabs of fixed time length.

Corollary
1.1

(rough version)
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
1.8
be satisfied.

For
T\geq 0
sufficiently large and for
\chi_{\textit{data}}(t^{\star})=\chi_{0,T^{2}}(t^{\star})
we have

(1.44)

\displaystyle\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}\mathbb{T}(n,n)[\psi]\lesssim\Bigg(\int_{\{t^{\star}=0\}}\mathbb{T}(n,n)[\psi]+\int_{\supp\chi_{\textit{data}}}d\tau^{\prime}\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau^{\prime}\}}\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{G}_{\chi_{\textit{data}}}\psi,\psi)\Bigg)\cdot e^{-c\tau},

\displaystyle\sup_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}|\psi-\underline{\psi}|\lesssim\sqrt{E}\cdot e^{-c\tau},

where
E
is an appropriate integral quantity defined on
\{t^{\star}=0\}
and
\underline{\psi}
is a constant satisfying
|\underline{\psi}|\leq|\underline{\psi}(0)|+C\sqrt{E}
if
\mu_{KG}=0
and
\underline{\psi}\equiv 0
if
\mu^{2}_{KG}>0
.

Proof.

See Section
9
.
∎

Remark 1.8
.

Note that we do not explicitly give the dependence of
\underline{\psi}
on initial data. Specifically, we note that the constant
\underline{\psi}
is obtained from a limiting argument, see already Section
9
.

Remark 1.9
.

For a ‘relatively non-degenerate’ estimate for the inhomogeneous wave equation see already Theorem
2
in Section
6
. Note that the estimate of Theorem
2
is used in our forthcoming
[
29
]
to prove global non-linear stability and exponential decay for the solutions of the quasilinear wave equation on the subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole background, if the condition (MS) holds.

2.
Preliminaries

In order for the present paper to be read independently, we repeat some necessary definitions and known results that have already been presented in our companion
[
28
]
.

2.1.
The subextremal parameters

We define the subextremal set of black hole parameters.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
l>0
. We define the set

(2.1)

\mathcal{B}_{l}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{(a,M)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty):\penalty\ \Delta\penalty\ \textrm{attains four distinct real roots}\},

where for
\Delta
see (
1.2
).

If
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
we say that the black hole parameters
(a,M,l)
correspond to a subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole and denote as

(2.2)

\bar{r}_{-}(a,M,l)\quad<\quad 0\quad\leq\quad r_{-}(a,M,l)\quad<\quad r_{+}(a,M,l)\quad<\quad\bar{r}_{+}(a,M,l)

the four distinct real roots of the
\Delta
polynomial. Note that in the Schwarzschild–de Sitter case
(a=0)
we have
r_{-}=0
.

For
(a,M,l)
be subextremal Kerr–de Sitter black hole parameters, see Definition
2.1
. Then, we have the following graphic representation of the polynomial
\Delta
.

Figure 3.
The
\Delta
polynomial with 4 roots

We have the following

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Then, the following hold

(2.3)

\displaystyle\frac{d\Delta}{dr}=2r-\frac{4}{l^{2}}r^{3}-a^{2}\frac{2}{l^{2}}r-2M,\qquad\frac{d^{2}\Delta}{dr^{2}}=2-a^{2}\frac{2}{l^{2}}-\frac{12}{l^{2}}r^{2},\qquad\frac{d^{3}\Delta}{dr^{3}}=-\frac{24}{l^{2}}r

(2.4)

\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r_{+})>0,\quad\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(\bar{r}_{+})<0,\quad\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}\right)(r_{+})>0,\quad\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}\right)(\bar{r}_{+})<0,

where for
\Delta
see (
1.2
).

2.2.
The Kerr–de Sitter manifold

We define the Kerr–de Sitter manifold to be

(2.5)

\mathcal{M}=[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]_{r}\times\mathbb{R}_{t^{\star}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{(\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})},

where we term the coordinates
(t^{\star},r,\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})
Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates, see our companion
[
28
]
for their definition. We define the event horizon and the cosmological horizon respectively as

(2.6)

\mathcal{H}^{+}=\{r=r_{+},\penalty\ t^{\star}>-\infty\},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}=\{r=\bar{r}_{+},\penalty\ t^{\star}>-\infty\}.

Note that the hypersurfaces
\mathcal{H}^{+},\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
are boundaries of the manifold
\mathcal{M}
. The spacelike hypersurfaces
\{t^{\star}=c\}
connect
\mathcal{H}^{+}
with
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
. We refer the reader to the Carter–Penrose diagram of Figure
4
.

Figure 4.
Carter-Penrose diagram of Kerr–de Sitter

2.3.
The metric in Boyer–Lindquist coordinates

To define the metric on
\mathcal{M}
it is useful to recall first the Boyer Lindquist coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
on the Kerr–de Sitter manifold from our companion [Section 2,
[
28
]
]. Specifically, note that
r
coincides with the
r
of the Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates above and
\theta=\theta^{\star}
. We need the following definition.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Then, we define

(2.7)

\displaystyle\rho^{2}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}

\displaystyle r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta,\qquad\Delta_{\theta}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}\cos^{2}\theta,\qquad\Xi\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu1+\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}.

We now define the Kerr–de Sitter metric in Boyer–Lindquist coordinates

Definition 2.3
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. We define the Kerr–de Sitter metric as

(2.8)

\displaystyle g_{a,M,l}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Delta}dr^{2}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{\Delta_{\theta}}d\theta^{2}+\frac{\Delta_{\theta}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-\Delta a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Xi^{2}\rho^{2}}\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi^{2}-2\frac{\Delta_{\theta}(r^{2}+a^{2})-\Delta}{\Xi\rho^{2}}a\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi dt

\displaystyle-\frac{\Delta-\Delta_{\theta}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\rho^{2}}dt^{2},

where for
\Delta
see (
1.2
), and for
\Xi,\rho^{2},\Delta_{\theta}
see Definition
2.2
. The metric (
2.8
) extends smoothly to
\mathcal{M}
and moreover the metric depends smoothly on
a,M
for fixed
l
.

The vector field
W=\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi}
is timelike away from the horizons, see already Lemma
2.2
. We time orient
\mathcal{M}
by the vector
W
.

Remark 2.1
.

We have that
\partial_{t}=\partial_{t^{\star}}
and
\partial_{\varphi}=\partial_{\varphi^{\star}}
. Thus, these Boyer Lindquist coordinate vector fields extend regularly at
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
, despite the fact that the coordinates themselves do not.

2.4.
A causal vector field for the entire subextremal Kerr–de Sitter family

We present Lemma
2.2
, proved in our companion
[
28
]
, which states that the vector field

(2.9)

W=\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi}

is timelike in
\mathcal{M}
except at the horizons
\mathcal{H}^{+},\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
where it is null. By Definition
2.3
, this then is future directed with respect to the time orientation it determines.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. The vector field

(2.10)

W\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\partial_{t}+\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi},

is timelike in
\{r_{+}<r<\bar{r}_{+}\}
and null on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and on the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
. Note that (
2.10
) is also timelike for
\Lambda=0
.

Moreover, for axisymmetric solutions
\psi
of the Klein–Gordon equation (
1.5
) the following holds

(2.11)

\rm{K}^{\partial_{t^{\star}}+\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi^{\star}}}[\psi]=0,

where for
\rm{K}^{X}
see already Section
2.10
.

Proof.

See our companion
[
28
]
.
∎

2.5.
The regular vector field
Z^{\star}

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Let
\partial_{r}|_{star}
be the coordinate vector field of the modified Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates
(t^{\star},r,\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})
, see
[
28
]
, and
\partial_{r}|_{\textit{BL}}
be the coordinate vector field of the Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
, see Definition
[
28
]
.

For a sufficiently small

(2.12)

\epsilon_{\textit{hyb}}(a,M,l)>0,

we choose a smooth cut-off that satisfies the following

(2.13)

\chi_{\textit{hyb}}(r)=\begin{cases}0,\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu&r\in(r_{+},r_{+}+\epsilon_{\textit{hyb}})\cup(\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon_{\textit{hyb}},\bar{r}_{+}),\\
1,\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu&r\in(r_{+}+2\epsilon_{\textit{hyb}},\bar{r}_{+}-2\epsilon_{\textit{hyb}}).\end{cases}

Then, we define the hybrid vector field

(2.14)

Z^{\star}=(1-\chi_{\textit{hyb}}(r))\partial_{r}|_{\textit{star}}+\chi_{\textit{hyb}}(r)\partial_{r}|_{\textit{BL}}.

The vector field
Z^{\star}
coincides with the coordinate vector field
\partial_{r}|_{BL}
, of Boyer–Lindquist coordinates, in the region
[R_{-},R_{+}]
, where for
R_{\pm}
see Theorem
4.1
. The significance of
Z^{\star}
will be that the derivatives
(Z^{\star}\psi)^{2}
do
not
degenerate in the integrand of the Morawetz estimate of Theorem
4.1
.

2.6.
Hawking–Reall vector fields

We define the null normals of the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
respectively as the Hawking–Reall vector fields

(2.15)

K^{+}=\partial_{t^{\star}}+\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi^{\star}},\qquad\bar{K}^{+}=\partial_{t^{\star}}+\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\varphi^{\star}}.

2.7.
Volume forms and normals

We denote by
n
the normals of the foliation
\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
. Moreover, when convenient we denote the normal vector fields (
2.15
) also as
n
. The context will make it clear what is meant.

We use the notation
\not{g},\penalty\ \not{\nabla},\penalty\ d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
to denote respectively the induced
g_{a,M,l}
metric on the
\mathbb{S}^{2}
factors of
\mathcal{M}
, the covariant derivative with respect to
\not{g}
and the standard metric of the unit sphere. Then, we have

(2.16)

\not{g}=r^{2}v(r,\theta)d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}},

with
v(r,\theta)\sim 1
in
\mathcal{M}
, where the unnecessary weights in
r
are included to compare with the asymptotically flat
\Lambda=0
Kerr black hole.

The spacetime volume form and the volume form of hypersurfaces
\{t^{\star}=c\}
are given respectively by

(2.17)

dg=v(r,\theta)dt^{\star}drd\not{g}=v(r,\theta)\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}dt^{\star}dr^{\star}d\not{g}=v(r,\theta)dtdrd\not{g},\qquad dg_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}=v(r,\theta)drd\theta^{\star}d\varphi^{\star},

where
\rho^{2}=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta
and for
\Delta
see (
1.2
), with
v(r,\theta)\sim 1
. We define the volume forms of the null hypersurfaces
\mathcal{H}^{+},\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
respectively as

(2.18)

dg=(K^{+})^{\flat}\wedge dg_{\mathcal{H}^{+}},\qquad dg=(\bar{K}^{+})^{\flat}\wedge dg_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}},

where
\flat
here is the flat-musical isomorphism with respect to the Kerr–de Sitter metric, see (
1.1
), and
K^{+},\bar{K}^{+}
are the Hawking–Reall vector fields of Section
2.6
.

2.8.
Causal domains

We define the following causal spacetime domains.

Definition 2.4
.

We define

(2.19)

D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{\tau_{1}\leq t^{\star}\leq\tau_{2}\},\qquad D(\tau_{1},\infty)\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{t^{\star}\geq\tau_{1}\},

where the spacelike hypersurfaces
\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
connect the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
with the cosmological horizon
\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
, see Figure
4
, or
[
28
]
for their precise definition.

In our Theorem
2
we will need the following extended manifold

Definition 2.5
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Let
\delta>0
be sufficiently small. We define the extended manifold

(2.20)

\mathcal{M}_{\delta}=[r_{+}-\delta,\bar{r}_{+}+\delta]_{r}\times\mathbb{R}_{t^{\star}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{(\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})},

with respect to the Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates
(t^{\star},r,\theta^{\star},\varphi^{\star})
see Section
2.2
. The Kerr–de Sitter metric (
2.8
) extends smoothly to
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
by its analytic expression near
r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}
in Kerr–de Sitter star coordinates (see
[
28
]
). Note that the hypersurfaces
\{t^{\star}=c\}
are spacelike. We define the spacelike boundaries of the manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
to be

(2.21)

\mathcal{H}^{+}_{\delta}=\{r=r_{+}-\delta,\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mut^{\star}>-\infty\},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}_{\delta}=\{r=\bar{r}_{+}+\delta,\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mut^{\star}>-\infty\}.

Now, we define the causal domains

(2.22)

D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{\tau_{1}\leq t^{\star}\leq\tau_{2}\}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\delta},\qquad D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},+\infty)\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\{t^{\star}\geq\tau_{1}\}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\delta}.

Remark 2.2
.

We will use the
\delta
-extended manifolds of Definition
2.5
in Theorem
2
.

2.9.
Coarea formula

Let
f
be a continuous non-negative function. Then, note the following holds

(2.23)

\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\frac{1}{r}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mufdg\sim\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}d\tau\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}fdg_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}.

(We have included the
r
factor so that the constant implicit in
\sim
does not degenerate as
l=\infty
).

2.10.
The energy momentum tensor and the divergence theorem

Definition 2.6
.

Let
g
be a smooth Lorentzian metric. The energy momentum tensor associated to a solution of the inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon equation

(2.24)

\Box_{g}\psi-\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}\psi=F,

where
F
is a sufficiently regular function, is defined as

(2.25)

\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[\psi]\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\partial_{\mu}\psi\partial_{\nu}\psi-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\Big(|\nabla\psi|_{g}^{2}+\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}|\psi|^{2}\Big),

where
|\nabla\psi|^{2}_{g}=g^{\gamma\delta}\partial_{\gamma}\psi\partial_{\delta}\psi
. The energy current, with respect to a vector field
X
, is defined as

(2.26)

J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[\psi]X^{\nu}.

Note that for
X,N
everywhere causal future directed vector fields, the following holds

(2.27)

J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]N^{\mu}=\mathbb{T}(X,N)[\psi]\geq 0.

The divergence of the energy current is

(2.28)

\nabla^{\mu}J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]=X(\psi)\cdot F+\rm{K}^{X}[\psi],

where

(2.29)

\displaystyle\rm{K}^{X}[\psi]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu^{(X)}\pi^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{\mu}\psi\partial_{\nu}\psi-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}|\nabla\psi|^{2}\right)\pi^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}|\psi|^{2}\Tr\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu^{(X)}\pi,\qquad^{(X)}\pi^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla^{\mu}X^{\nu}+\nabla^{\nu}X^{\mu}\big).

The following is the divergence theorem.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Let
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
. Let
\psi
satisfy the inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon equation

(2.30)

\Box_{g}\psi-\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}\psi=F,

on
D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
where
g=g_{a,M,l}
.

Then, the following holds

(2.31)

\displaystyle\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}}J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+\int_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\rm{K}^{X}[\psi]

\displaystyle=\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}-\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}X\psi\cdot F.

Note that the integrals and normals of (
2.31
) are to be understood with respect to the volume forms and normals of Sections
2.7
,
2.6
. A similar statement holds for
D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
.

For a further study of currents related to partial differential equations, see the monograph of Christodoulou
[
8
]
.

2.11.
Time cutoffs

Let
T\geq 3
and
T\leq T+\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}-T\leq\tau_{2}
. We choose smooth cut-offs that will satisfy the following

(2.32)

\chi^{(T)}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}(t^{\star})\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu=\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\begin{cases}\text{1,}&\quad\{\tau_{1}+T\leq t^{\star}\leq\tau_{2}-T\}\\
\text{0,}&\quad\{t^{\star}\leq\tau_{1}\}\cup\{t^{\star}\geq\tau_{2}\},\\
\end{cases}\qquad\eta^{(T)}_{\tau_{1}}(t^{\star})\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu=\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\begin{cases}\text{1,}&\quad\{t^{\star}\geq T+\tau_{1}\}\\
\text{0,}&\quad\{t^{\star}\leq\tau_{1}\}\\
\end{cases}

which we often simply denote as
\chi_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}},\penalty\ \eta_{\tau_{1}}
respectively. We also require that
|Z^{\star}\eta|+|\partial_{t^{\star}}\eta|+|\not{\nabla}\eta|\leq\frac{B}{T}
and
|\partial_{t^{\star}}^{2}\eta|\leq\frac{B}{T^{2}}
, with respect to the Kerr–de Sitter–star coordinates, for a constant
B
independent of
T,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
. Also see Figure
5
.

Figure 5.
The cut-off (
2.32
)

We define the cut-offed functions

(2.33)

\psi_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}(t^{\star},r,\varphi^{\star},\theta)\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\chi^{(T)}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}(t^{\star})\psi(t^{\star},r,\varphi^{\star},\theta),\qquad\psi_{\tau_{1}}(t^{\star},r,\varphi^{\star},\theta)\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\eta^{(T)}_{\tau_{1}}(t^{\star})\psi(t^{\star},r,\varphi^{\star},\theta).

For
T\geq 3
, let
\tilde{T}\leq\tau_{1}
, where for
\tilde{T}
see (
1.38
), and define

(2.34)

\chi_{+}=\chi^{(T)}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{1}+T^{2}}(t^{\star}),\qquad\chi_{-}(t^{\star})=\chi^{(T)}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{1}+T^{2}}\left(t^{\star}+\tilde{T}\right),

also see Figure
2
. We also require that
|\frac{d}{dt^{\star}}\chi_{+}|+|\frac{d}{dt^{\star}}\chi_{-}|\leq\frac{B}{T}
.

2.12.
Tortoise coordinate

We define the tortoise coordinate

(2.35)

\frac{dr^{\star}}{dr}=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta(r)},

where for
\Delta(r)
see (
1.2
), with
r^{\star}(r_{\Delta,\textit{frac}})=0
, where

(2.36)

r_{\Delta,\textit{frac}}\in(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})

is the unique local maximum of

(2.37)

\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}},

also see our companion
[
28
]
. Note that
r^{\star}(r)
is a diffeomorphism mapping
(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})\rightarrow(-\infty,+\infty)

Note that we will often use the primed notation

(2.38)

{}^{\prime}=\frac{d}{dr^{\star}}

for the
r^{\star}
-derivative. Finally, for a value
\alpha\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]
, we will often use the notation

(2.39)

\alpha^{\star}=r^{\star}(\alpha),

where note
r_{+}^{\star}=-\infty,\penalty\ \bar{r}_{+}^{\star}=+\infty
.

2.13.
Poincare–Wirtinger inequality

Note the following Lemma

Lemma 2.4
.

Assume that
\psi
is a smooth function on the manifold
\mathcal{M}
. Then, for any
\tau\geq 0
we have the following

(2.40)

\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}|\psi-\underline{\psi}(\tau)|^{2}\leq B\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}

where
\underline{\psi}(\tau)=\frac{1}{|\{t^{\star}=\tau\}|}\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}\psi
and
|\{t^{\star}=\tau\}|=\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau\}}1
with respect to the volume forms of Section
2.7
.

3.
The
(t,\varphi)
Fourier projections and Carter’s separation in Kerr–de Sitter

Let
l>0
,
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. In the present Section we present the Fourier projections and Carter’s separation of variables we will use in the present paper.

3.1.
Fourier decomposition in the
t,\varphi
directions

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R},m\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
be the Boyer–Lindquist coordinates. Suppose that
\Psi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function with
\supp\Psi\subset\{0\leq t^{\star}<\infty\}
. We define the following Fourier transform

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\omega,m}(\Psi)(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{-i\omega t}e^{im\varphi}\Psi(t,r,\theta,\varphi)d\varphi dt,

where note that the integral (
3.1
) converges pointwise.

Throughout this paper we will use the Fourier trasform (
3.1
) with respect to the Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
t,\varphi
, for test functions
\Psi
whose support is a subset of
\{0\leq t^{\star}<\infty\}
. For the Kerr–de Sitter star coordinate
t^{\star}
see Section
2.2
. The Fourier decomposition in the
t,\varphi
directions is also used to define the pseudodifferential operator
\mathcal{G}
, see Definition
1.4
, that is the central object of the present paper.

3.2.
The superradiant frequencies

An explicit computation shows that the
\bar{K}^{+}
energy flux along the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is given by

(3.2)

\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}K_{+}\psi\overline{\bar{K}^{+}\psi}=\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\left(\partial_{t}\psi+\omega_{+}\partial_{\varphi}\psi\right)\cdot\overline{\left(\partial_{t}\psi+\bar{\omega}_{+}\partial_{\varphi}\psi\right)},

where for the Hawking–Reall vector fields
K^{+},\bar{K}^{+}
see Section
2.6
. In particular, if we consider a solution of the form
\psi=e^{-i\omega t}e^{im\varphi}\psi_{0}(r,\theta)
then the sign of (
3.2
) is formally determined by the sign of

(3.3)

\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right).

Therefore, the superradiant frequencies are defined to be the frequencies for which (
3.2
) is negative. Namely, the superradiant frequencies are

(3.4)

\mathcal{SF}:=\Bigl\{(\omega,m):\penalty\ \left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)<0\Bigr\},

where note that for the frequencies (
3.4
) the left hand side of (
3.22
) is not coercive.

We note that the superradiant frequency set (
3.4
) is also equal to

(3.5)

\Bigl\{(\omega,m):\penalty\ am\omega\in\left(\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\Bigr\}.

3.3.
Carter’s separation

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq|m|}
and let
S^{(a\omega),\mu_{KG}}_{m\ell},\lambda^{(a\omega),\mu_{KG}}_{m\ell}
be respectively the spheroidal harmonics and their eigenvalues, as discussed in our companion
[
28
]
. For brevity we denote them simply as

(3.6)

S^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell},\qquad\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell},

dropping the dependence on
\mu_{KG}
.

We need the following definition

Definition 3.1
.

Let
l>0
,
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. We fix
0<\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\infty
. Let
\chi(t^{\star})
be any smooth cut-off such that

(3.7)

\supp\chi\subset\{\tau_{1}\leq t^{\star}\leq\tau_{2}\}.

Let
F
be a sufficiently regular function. Let
\psi
be smooth solution of the inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon equation

(3.8)

\Box_{g_{a,M,l}}\psi-\mu^{2}_{KG}\psi=F

holds. Then, for

(3.9)

\psi_{\chi}=\chi\psi

we have

(3.10)

\Box_{g}\psi_{\chi}-\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}\psi_{\chi}=F_{0},\qquad F_{0}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu2\nabla^{b}\chi\nabla_{b}\psi+\psi\Box_{g}(\chi)+\chi F,

and for all
(\omega,m,\ell)
we define the following smooth Fourier transformations

(3.11)

\displaystyle\Psi^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}e^{-i\omega t}e^{im\varphi}S^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\cdot\psi_{\chi}(t,r,\theta,\varphi)d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}dt,

\displaystyle(F_{0})^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}e^{-i\omega t}e^{im\varphi}S^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\cdot F_{0}(t,r,\theta,\varphi)d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}dt,

where
d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
is the usual volume form on the unit sphere. The integrals on the RHS of (
3.11
) converge in a pointwise sense for
r_{+}<r<\bar{r}_{+}
.

We have the following Proposition

Proposition 3.1

(Carter’s separation)
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. Let
\psi,F
be smooth functions in
\{0\leq t^{\star}<\infty\}
and such that the inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon equation

(3.12)

\Box_{g_{a,M,l}}\psi-\mu^{2}_{KG}\psi=F

holds.

Let
\chi,\psi_{\chi},F_{0},\Psi^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}
be as in Definition
3.1
, where specifically recall that
\supp\chi\subset\{0\leq t^{\star}<\infty\}
. Then, for all
(\omega,m,\ell)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq|m|}
the rescaled radial fixed frequency function

(3.13)

u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}(r)=\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Psi^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}(r)

is smooth in
r\in(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
, and moreover it satisfies Carter’s fixed frequency radial ode

(3.14)

[u^{(a\omega)}_{ml}(r)]^{\prime\prime}+(\omega^{2}-V^{(a\omega)}_{ml}(r))u^{(a\omega)}_{ml}(r)=H^{(a\omega)}_{ml}(r),\qquad H^{(a\omega)}_{ml}=\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3/2}}\left(\rho^{2}F_{0}\right)^{(a\omega)}_{ml}(r),

with
{}^{\prime}=\frac{d}{dr^{\star}}
, see Section
2.12
.

The potential
V
can we written as

(3.15)

V\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0muV^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}(r)=V_{\textit{SL}}(r)+\left(V_{0}\right)^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}(r)+V_{\mu_{\textit{KG}}}(r),

where

(3.16)

\displaystyle V_{\textit{SL}}

\displaystyle=(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1/2}\left(\frac{d}{dr^{\star}}\right)^{2}(\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}})

\displaystyle=-\Delta^{2}\frac{3r^{2}}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{4}}+\Delta\frac{-5\frac{r^{4}}{l^{2}}+3r^{2}(1-\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}})-4Mr+a^{2}}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}},

\displaystyle V_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta(\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}+\omega^{2}a^{2})-\Xi^{2}a^{2}m^{2}-2m\omega a\Xi(\Delta-(r^{2}+a^{2}))}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}(\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}+a^{2}\omega^{2}-2am\omega\Xi)+\omega^{2}-\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle V_{\mu_{\textit{KG}}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\textit{KG}}^{2}\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}.

Moreover, the following boundary conditions hold

(3.17)

\displaystyle\frac{du}{dr^{\star}}=-i(\omega-\omega_{+}m)u,\qquad\frac{du}{dr^{\star}}=i(\omega-\bar{\omega}_{+}m)u,

at
r^{\star}=-\infty,\penalty\ r^{\star}=+\infty
respectively, in the sense

(3.18)

\lim_{r^{\star}\rightarrow-\infty}\left(u^{\prime}(r^{\star})+i(\omega-\omega_{+}m)u(r^{\star})\right)=0,\qquad\lim_{r^{\star}\rightarrow+\infty}\left(u^{\prime}(r^{\star})-i(\omega-\bar{\omega}_{+}m)u(r^{\star})\right)=0

where
\omega_{+}=\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\penalty\ \bar{\omega}_{+}=\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
. Finally, we note that
|u|^{2}(\pm\infty)=\lim_{r^{\star}\rightarrow\pm\infty}|u|^{2}(r^{\star})
are well defined.

Remark 3.1
.

Note that in Proposition
3.1
we may also cut-off the solution of the Klein–Gordon equation with a cut-off that does not vanish in the future of
\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}
. However, in order to do that we need to restrict to a class of solutions to Klein–Gordon that are ‘sufficiently integrable’ in an
L^{2}
sense, see our companion
[
28
]
.

We need the following definition

Definition 3.2
.

Let
l>0
,
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R},m\in\mathbb{Z},\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq|m|}
. Let
\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}
be as in (
3.6
). Then, we define the following

(3.19)

\tilde{\lambda}^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}=\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}+a^{2}\omega^{2}.

We will often drop the frequency dependence and denote (
3.19
) simply as
\tilde{\lambda}
.

3.4.
Parseval identities

We have the following Proposition

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
l>0
,
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Morever, let
\chi,\psi,\psi_{\chi}
be as in Definition
3.1
. Then, we have the following Parseval identities

(3.20)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{t}(\psi_{\chi})|^{2}\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{m,\ell}\omega^{2}|\Psi^{(a\omega)}_{m,\ell}|^{2}d\omega,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{\varphi}(\psi_{\chi})|^{2}\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{m,\ell}m^{2}|\Psi^{(a\omega)}_{m,\ell}|^{2}d\omega,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{r^{\star}}(\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}\psi_{\chi})|^{2}\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{m,\ell}|\partial_{r^{\star}}\left(\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Psi^{(a\omega)}_{m,\ell}\right)|^{2}d\omega,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\psi_{\chi}|^{2}\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{m,\ell}|\Psi^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}|^{2}d\omega,

for any
r_{+}<r<\bar{r}_{+}
, and

(3.21)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{m,\ell}\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}|\Psi^{(a\omega)}_{m,\ell}|^{2}d\omega=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|^{d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}}\nabla(\psi_{\chi})|_{d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}}^{2}\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi dt-a^{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{t}(\psi_{\chi})|^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi dt,

for any
r_{+}<r<\bar{r}_{+}
, where
{}^{d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}}\nabla
is the covariant derivative of
d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
and
d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
is the standard metric of the unit sphere.

3.5.
Energy identity for smooth outgoing solutions of (
3.14
)

Note the following

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
l>0
,
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R},m\in\mathbb{Z},\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq|m|}
. Let
u
be a smooth solution of Carter’s radial ode (
3.14
) which moreover satisfies the boundary conditions (
3.17
). Then, we have the following

(3.22)

\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)|u|^{2}(\infty)+\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)|u|^{2}(-\infty)=\imaginary(\bar{u}H).

Proof.

This can also be found in our companion
[
31
]
. We multiply the radial ode (
3.14
) with
\bar{u}
and after integration by parts we obtain the desired result, in view of the outgoing boundary conditions (
3.17
).
∎

3.6.
The fixed frequency current
Q^{h}

We will need the following current

Definition 3.3
.

Let
l>0
,
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
. Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R},m\in\mathbb{Z},\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq|m|}
. Let
v:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a
C^{0}(-\infty,+\infty)
function and let
v,v^{\prime}
be piecewise
C^{1}(-\infty,\infty)
. Moreover, assume that
u
is a distributional solution of the inhomogeneous Carter’s radial ode
v^{\prime\prime}(r^{\star})+(\omega^{2}-V)v(r^{\star})=H
, where for the potential
V
see Proposition
3.1
, and the inhomogeneity
H
is a distribution.

Then, given a smooth function
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we define the current

(3.23)

\displaystyle Q^{h}[v]

\displaystyle\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0muh\real(v^{\prime}\bar{v})-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}|v|^{2}.

The following identity holds in a distributional sense

(3.24)

\displaystyle(Q^{h}[v])^{\prime}

\displaystyle=h|v^{\prime}|^{2}+\Big(h(V-\omega^{2})-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime\prime}\Big)|v|^{2}+h\real(v\bar{H}).

4.
The Morawetz estimates of
[
28
]

Before presenting the main results of
[
28
]
that we need in the present paper, see already Theorems
4.1
, we need first to discuss some preliminary definitions and notation that have already been presented in
[
28
]
.

4.1.
Preliminaries

We need the following definition

Definition 4.1
.

Let
l>0
and
\mu^{2}_{KG}\geq 0
.
We define the sets

(4.1)

\widetilde{\mathcal{MS}}_{l,\mu_{KG}}=\{(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}:\omega\in\mathbb{R},m\in\mathbb{Z},\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq|m|},\omega\neq\omega_{+}m,\omega\neq\bar{\omega}_{+}m\penalty\ \penalty\ |\mathcal{W}^{-1}(a,M,l,\mu^{2}_{KG},\omega,m,\ell)|<+\infty\},

where
\omega_{+}=\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\bar{\omega}_{+}=\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
, and

(4.2)

\mathcal{B}_{0,l}=\{(0,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}\}=\{(0,M):\penalty\ 0<\frac{M^{2}}{l^{2}}<\frac{1}{27}\},

where recall from our companion
[
28
]
that
\mathcal{B}_{0,l}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{MS}}_{l}
.

We define the following set

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{MS}_{l,\mu_{KG}}

to be the connected component of
\mathcal{B}_{0,l}
in the set
\widetilde{\mathcal{MS}}_{l,\mu_{KG}}
with the standard euclidean topology.

4.2.
The Morawetz estimate of
[
28
]

Let
T,\tau_{1}\geq 0
and let
\chi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be as follows

(4.4)

\chi=\eta^{(T)}_{\tau_{1}}\chi^{2}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{1}+T^{2}}

where for the smooth cut-offs
\eta^{(T)}_{\tau_{1}},\chi_{\tau_{1},\tau_{1}+T^{2}}
see Section
2.11
. For convenience we will denote
\eta_{\tau_{1}}^{(T)}
as
\eta
and
\chi_{\tau_{1},\tau_{1}+T^{2}}
as
\chi_{+}
.

Remark 4.1
.

Note we do not require that the cut-off (
4.4
) vanishes in the future of
\{t^{\star}\geq\tau_{1}+T^{2}\}
.

We have

Theorem 4.1

(Main Theorem of
[
28
]
)
.

Let
l>0
,
\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}\geq 0
and

(a,M)\in\mathcal{MS}_{l,\mu_{KG}},

where for
\mathcal{MS}_{l,\mu_{KG}}
see (
4.3
).

Then, there exist constants

(4.5)

C=C(a,M,l,\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}})>0,\qquad r_{+}<R^{-}\leq R^{+}<\bar{r}_{+}

and a frequency dependent parameter

(4.6)

r_{trap}(\omega,m,\ell)\in(R^{-},R^{+})\cup\{0\}

such that the following holds.

Let
T>0,1\leq\tau_{1}<\tau_{1}+1\leq\tau_{2}
and let
\psi
be a smooth solution of the inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon equation
\Box_{g_{a,M,l}}\psi-\mu^{2}_{KG}\psi=F
in
D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
, and let
u
be the projection in frequencies of
\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}\chi\psi
, as in Proposition
3.1
. For the cut-off
\chi
see (
4.4
) and also see [Remark 7.1,
[
28
]
].

Then, we have the following

(4.7)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}d\omega\sum_{m,\ell}\Bigg(\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{+}+\epsilon}\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}|u^{\prime}+i(\omega-\omega_{+}m)u|^{2}dr+\int_{\bar{r}_{+}-\epsilon}^{\bar{r}_{+}}\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}|u^{\prime}-(\omega-\bar{\omega}_{+}m)u|^{2}dr

\displaystyle+\int_{r_{+}}^{\bar{r}_{+}}\left(1_{\{|m|>0\}}|u|^{2}+\mu^{2}_{\textit{KG}}|u|^{2}+|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\left(1-\frac{r_{\textit{trap}}(\omega,m,\ell)}{r}\right)^{2}(\omega^{2}+\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell})|u|^{2}\right)dr\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+C\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{t}\psi|^{2}+\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{\varphi}\psi|^{2}+(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}|F|^{2},

(4.8)

\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}\leq C\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+C\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{t}\psi|^{2}+\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{\varphi}\psi|^{2}+(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}|F|^{2},

(4.9)

\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+|\psi|^{2}\leq C\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}+|\psi|^{2}+C\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{t}\psi|^{2}+\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{\varphi}\psi|^{2}+(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}|F|^{2},

(4.10)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\infty)}J^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}+\int_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\infty)}J^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}_{\{t^{\star}=0\}}+C\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{t}\psi|^{2}+\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{\varphi}\psi|^{2}+(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}|F|^{2},

(4.11)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\infty)}J^{N}_{\mu}[\chi\psi]n^{\mu}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}+\int_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}\cap D(\tau_{1},\infty)}J^{N}_{\mu}[\chi\psi]n^{\mu}_{\bar{\mathcal{H}}^{+}}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}J^{n}_{\mu}[\psi]n^{\mu}_{\{t^{\star}=0\}}+|\psi|^{2}+C\int\int_{D(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{t}\psi|^{2}+\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{\varphi}\psi|^{2}+(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}|F|^{2},

for a sufficiently small
\epsilon(a,M,l)>0
and for any
0<\epsilon^{\prime}<1
, where
\omega_{+}=\frac{a\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\penalty\ \bar{\omega}_{+}=\frac{a\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
.

Remark 4.2
.

The last estimate (
4.11
) was not included in our companion
[
28
]
, but can easily be inferred from the estimates (
4.7
)-(
4.10
).

(4.13)

\displaystyle\int\int_{D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},\infty)}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}+i_{2}\leq j}|\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\penalty\hskip 0.71562pt}^{i_{1}}(\partial_{t^{\star}})^{i_{2}}\mathcal{P}_{trap}(\chi\psi)|^{2}+\int\int_{D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},\infty)\setminus D(\tau_{1},\infty)}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\leq j}|\not{\nabla}^{i_{1}}\partial_{t}^{i_{2}}(Z^{\star})^{i_{3}}\psi|^{2}

\displaystyle+\int\int_{D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},\infty)}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\leq j-1}\left(|\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\penalty\hskip 0.71562pt}^{i_{1}}(\partial_{t^{\star}})^{i_{2}}(Z^{\star})^{i_{3}+1}\psi|^{2}+|\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\penalty\hskip 0.71562pt}^{i_{1}}(\partial_{t^{\star}})^{i_{2}}(Z^{\star})^{i_{3}}\psi|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}\sum_{0\leq i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\leq j}|\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\penalty\hskip 0.71562pt}^{i_{1}}(\partial_{t^{\star}})^{i_{2}}(Z^{\star})^{i_{3}}\psi|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}\sum_{0\leq i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\leq j}|\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\penalty\hskip 0.71562pt}^{i_{1}}(\partial_{t^{\star}})^{i_{2}}(Z^{\star})^{i_{3}}\psi|^{2}

\displaystyle+C\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq j}\int\int_{D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},\infty)}\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{t}^{i_{1}}\psi|^{2}+(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}|\partial_{t}^{i_{1}-1}F|^{2}+\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{\varphi}^{i_{1}}\psi|^{2}+(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}|\partial_{\varphi}^{i_{1}-1}F|^{2},

(4.15)

\displaystyle\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{2}\}}\sum_{0\leq i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\leq j}|\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\penalty\hskip 0.71562pt}^{i_{1}}(\partial_{t^{\star}})^{i_{2}}(Z^{\star})^{i_{3}}\psi|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\{t^{\star}=\tau_{1}\}}\sum_{0\leq i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}\leq j}|\mbox{$\nabla\mkern-13.0mu/$\penalty\hskip 0.71562pt}^{i_{1}}(\partial_{t^{\star}})^{i_{2}}(Z^{\star})^{i_{3}}\psi|^{2}

\displaystyle+C\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq j}\int\int_{D_{\delta}(\tau_{1},\infty)}\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{t}^{i_{1}}\psi|^{2}+(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}|\partial_{t}^{i_{1}-1}F|^{2}+\epsilon^{\prime}|\partial_{\varphi}^{i_{1}}\psi|^{2}+(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}|\partial_{\varphi}^{i_{1}-1}F|^{2},

for any

. We used the operator

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\textit{trap}}[\Psi]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{m,\ell}\left|1-\frac{r_{\textit{trap}}}{r}\right|e^{i\omega t}\Psi^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}(r)S^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}(\theta)e^{-im\phi}d\omega.

5.
The operator
\mathcal{G}

The main definition of this section is Definition
1
where we define the operator
\mathcal{G}
. However, first we need several preliminary definitions and lemmata to justify that
\mathcal{G}
is well defined.

5.1.
The definitions of
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r),g_{1}(r),g_{2}^{2}(\omega,m,r)

Note the following definition

Definition 5.1
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Let
(\omega,m)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
. We define

(5.1)

\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r)=\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}{\Delta}\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right)^{2},

and moreover we define

(5.2)

\displaystyle g_{1}(r)\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\sqrt{\Delta}},\qquad\left(g_{2}(\omega,m,r)\right)^{2}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r)-\min_{r\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]}\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r).

Remark 5.1
.

The function
\mathcal{T}
, see (
5.1
), has also been studied by Dyatlov for the case of the slowly rotating Kerr–de Sitter black hole, see
[
13
]
, and more recently by Petersen–Vasy for the entire subextremal range of Kerr–de Sitter black hole paremeters, see
[
34
]
.

In order to define
g_{2}(\omega,m,r)
, see already Definition
5.2
, we need first Propositions
5.1
,
5.2
.

5.2.
The critical point
r_{crit}(\omega,m)

In this Section, amongst other things, we will prove that for any
(\omega,m)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
the function
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,\cdot)
, see (
5.1
), attains a unique critical point
r_{crit}(\omega,m)
, a minimum.

First, we study
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r)
, see (
5.1
), for the superradiant frequencies.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. For the superradiant frequencies

(5.3)

(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{SF}

the function
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r)
, see (
5.1
), satisfies
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,\cdot)\in C^{\infty}(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
, and it attains a unique critical point

(5.4)

r_{\textit{crit}}(\omega,m)\in(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}),

a minimum where
\frac{d\mathcal{T}}{dr}(r_{crit})=0
. The following holds

r_{crit}(\omega,m)=r_{s}(\omega,m),

where
am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{s}^{2}+a^{2}}
.

Moreover, we have that

(5.5)

\left|\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}\Big|_{r=r_{\textit{crit}}}\mathcal{T}\right|>0.

Proof.

For
(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{SF}
we note that there exists an
r_{s}\in(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
such that

(5.6)

am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{s}^{2}+a^{2}}.

Therefore, the derivative of
\mathcal{T}
, see (
5.1
), can be written as

(5.7)

\frac{d\mathcal{T}}{dr}=\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}{\Delta}\left(\omega-\frac{a\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}m\right)^{2}\right)=(r^{2}-r_{s}^{2})\frac{4r\Delta-(r^{2}-r_{s}^{2})\frac{d\Delta}{dr}}{2\Delta\sqrt{\Delta}}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu(r^{2}-r_{s}^{2})\frac{N_{2}(r;r_{s})}{2\Delta\sqrt{\Delta}},

where note that for any
r_{s}\in(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
the function
N_{2}(r;r_{s})
is a polynomial of degree
3
, namely

(5.8)

N_{2}(r;r_{s})=2\left((1-\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}-2\frac{r_{s}^{2}}{l^{2}})r^{3}-3Mr^{2}+(2a^{2}+r_{s}^{2}-\frac{a^{2}}{l^{2}}r_{s}^{2})r-2Mr_{s}^{2}\right).

From the definition of
N_{2}(r;r_{s})
, see (
5.7
), we note that

(5.9)

N_{2}(r_{+};r_{s})=-\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r_{+})(r_{+}^{2}-r_{s}^{2})>0,\qquad N_{2}(\bar{r}_{+};r_{s})=-\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(\bar{r}_{+})(\bar{r}_{+}^{2}-r_{s}^{2})>0.

Moreover, we note that for
r_{s}=\bar{r}_{+}
the following hold

(5.10)

N_{2}(\bar{r}_{+};\bar{r}_{+})=0,\quad\frac{dN_{2}}{dr}(\bar{r}_{+};\bar{r}_{+})=2\bar{r}_{+}\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(\bar{r}_{+})<0,\quad\frac{d^{2}N_{2}}{dr^{2}}(\bar{r}_{+};\bar{r}_{+})=6\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(\bar{r}_{+})<0.

Therefore, in view of (
5.9
), (
5.10
), we conclude that the 3rd degree polynomial
N_{2}(r;\bar{r}_{+})
attains three real roots
r_{1},r_{2},\bar{r}_{+}
that satisfy

(5.11)

r_{1}\leq r_{+}<\bar{r}_{+}<r_{2}

and

(5.12)

N_{2}(r;\bar{r}_{+})\geq 0,\penalty\ r\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]

where the only zero at
[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]
is attained at
r=\bar{r}_{+}
. Also, see Figure
6
.

Figure 6.
The function
N_{2}(r;\bar{r}_{+})

For the sake of contradiction

(5.13)

5.15
), we obtain that

(5.20)

\tilde{r}\leq r_{s,h}.

Moreover, we calculate

(5.21)

\frac{d^{2}N_{2}}{dr^{2}}(r_{s,h})=\left(6\frac{d\Delta}{dr}\right)(r=r_{s,h})<0.

Therefore, we obtain also that
r_{s,h}<\tilde{r}
, also see Figure
8
. This is a
contradiction
to (
5.20
).

Therefore, via a contradiction argument we have concluded that the polynomial
N_{2}(r;r_{s})
can only attain the zeros

(5.22)

r=r_{+},\qquad r=\bar{r}_{+}

respectively for the cases
r_{s}=r_{+},\penalty\ r_{s}=\bar{r}_{+}
. We conclude that in
[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]
the only critical point of the function
\mathcal{T}(r,\omega,m)
is attained at

(5.23)

r=r_{s}.

It is immediate to conclude inequality (
5.5
) for
am\omega\in\left(\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\right)
since
\frac{d\mathcal{T}}{dr}
attains a unique zero with multiplicity 1.
∎

Second, we study
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r)
for the non-superradiant frequencies

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
l>0
and
(a,M)\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. For the non-superradiant frequencies

(5.24)

(\omega,m)\in(\mathcal{SF})^{c}

the function
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r)
, see (
5.1
), satisfies
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,\cdot)\in C^{\infty}(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
. Moreover, the function
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,\cdot)
attains a unique critical point

(5.25)

r_{\textit{crit}}(\omega,m)\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}],

where
\frac{d\mathcal{T}}{dr}(r_{crit})=0
. The cases
r_{crit}=r_{+},r_{crit}=\bar{r}_{+}
correspond to the borderline superradiant cases
am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
and
am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
respectively, in which case we have
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,\cdot)\in C^{1}[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
and
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,\cdot)\in C^{1}(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]
respectively.

Moreover, for any
(\omega,m)\in(\mathcal{SF})^{c}\setminus\{am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}\}
we have that
\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,\cdot)\in C^{2}(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
and the following holds

(5.26)

\left|\frac{d^{2}}{dr^{2}}\Big|_{r=r_{\textit{crit}}}\mathcal{T}\right|>0.

For the borderline non superradiant frequencies
am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
or
am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
the function
\frac{d\mathcal{T}}{dr}
is not differentiable in
r
at the points
r=r_{+}
and
r=\bar{r}_{+}
, respectively.

Proof.

First, it is clear from (
5.1
) that
\mathcal{T}\in C^{\infty}(r_{+},\bar{r}_{+})
. For the borderline non superradiant frequencies
am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
we have respectively

(5.27)

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathcal{T}}{dr}

\displaystyle=(r^{2}-r_{+}^{2})\frac{4r\Delta-(r^{2}-r_{+}^{2})\frac{d\Delta}{dr}}{2\Delta\sqrt{\Delta}}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu(r^{2}-r_{+}^{2})\frac{N_{2}(r;r_{+})}{2\Delta\sqrt{\Delta}},

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathcal{T}}{dr}

\displaystyle=(r^{2}-\bar{r}_{+}^{2})\frac{4r\Delta-(r^{2}-r_{s}^{2})\frac{d\Delta}{dr}}{2\Delta\sqrt{\Delta}}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu\dot{=}\mskip 4.0mu plus 2.0mu minus 4.0mu(r^{2}-\bar{r}_{+}^{2})\frac{N_{2}(r;\bar{r}_{+})}{2\Delta\sqrt{\Delta}}.

It is easy to adapt the study of the polynomial
N_{2}(r;r_{s})
, of Proposition
5.1
, to prove that
N_{2}(r;r_{+}),N_{2}(r;\bar{r}_{+})
attain unique zeroes respectively at
r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}
. Moreover, it is obvious from (
5.27
) that the function
\frac{d\mathcal{T}}{dr}
is not differentiable for the frequencies
am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},am\omega=\frac{a^{2}m^{2}\Xi}{\bar{r}_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
at the values
r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}
respectively, but it is continous at
r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}
.

We note that if
\omega=0
then

(5.28)

\mathcal{T}(0,m,r)=\frac{(am\Xi)^{2}}{\Delta},

and therefore the Lemma follows by the properties of
\Delta
, see Lemma
2.1
and the subextremality condition of Definition
2.1
. Therefore, in what follows we assume that
\omega\not{=}0
.

We study

(5.29)

(\mathcal{T}(\omega,m,r))^{-1}=\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}\left(\omega-\frac{am\Xi}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right)^{2}}.

For the non-superradiant frequencies (
5.24
) we note that for all
r\in[r_{+},\bar{r}_{+}]
we have

(5.30)
